<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Saint Gregory" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Saint_Gregory_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Saint Gregory (disambiguation)">Saint Gregory (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

Gregory</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Gregory I</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Gregorius I</i>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;540</span> – 12 March 604), commonly known as <b>Saint Gregory the Great</b>, was the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Rome">bishop of Rome</a> from 3 September 590 to his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TheGreat_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TheGreat-2">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> He is known for instituting the first recorded large-scale mission from Rome, the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_mission" title="Gregorian mission">Gregorian mission</a>, to convert the then largely <a href="/wiki/Pagan" class="mw-redirect" title="Pagan">pagan</a> Anglo-Saxons to Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFlechner201547_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlechner201547-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Gregory is also well known for his writings, which were more prolific than those of any of his predecessors as pope.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The epithet <b>Saint Gregory the Dialogist</b> has been attached to him in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a> because of his <i><a href="/wiki/Dialogues_(Pope_Gregory)" class="mw-redirect" title="Dialogues (Pope Gregory)">Dialogues</a></i>. English translations of Eastern texts sometimes list him as Gregory "Dialogos", or the Anglo-Latinate equivalent "Dialogus".<sup id="cite_ref-OCA_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCA-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A <a href="/wiki/Roman_senator" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman senator">Roman senator</a>'s son and himself the <a href="/wiki/Prefect_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Prefect of Rome">prefect of Rome</a> at 30, Gregory lived in a <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monastery</a> he established on his family estate before becoming a papal ambassador and then pope. Although he was the first pope from a <a href="/wiki/Monastic" class="mw-redirect" title="Monastic">monastic</a> background, his prior political experiences may have helped him to be a talented administrator. During his papacy, his administration greatly surpassed that of the emperors in improving the welfare of the people of Rome, and he challenged the theological views of <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Eutychius_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Eutychius of Constantinople">Patriarch Eutychius of Constantinople</a> before the emperor <a href="/wiki/Tiberius_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Tiberius II">Tiberius II</a>. Gregory regained papal authority in <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Spain" title="Catholic Church in Spain">Spain</a> and <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_France" title="Catholic Church in France">France</a> and sent missionaries to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Church in England">England</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Canterbury" title="Augustine of Canterbury">Augustine of Canterbury</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paulinus_of_York" title="Paulinus of York">Paulinus of York</a>. The realignment of barbarian allegiance to Rome from their <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arian</a> Christian alliances shaped medieval Europe. Gregory saw <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lombards" title="Lombards">Lombards</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Visigoths" title="Visigoths">Visigoths</a> align with Rome in religion. He also combated the <a href="/wiki/Donatist" class="mw-redirect" title="Donatist">Donatist</a> heresy, popular particularly in North Africa at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-OCA_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCA-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout the Middle Ages, he was known as "the Father of Christian Worship" because of his exceptional efforts in revising the Roman worship of his day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllard1948125_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllard1948125-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His contributions to the development of the <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy_of_the_Presanctified_Gifts" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts">Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts</a>, still in use in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a>, were so significant that he is generally recognized as its <i>de facto</i> author.
</p><p>Gregory is one of the <a href="/wiki/Latin_Fathers" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin Fathers">Latin Fathers</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELivingstone1997415_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELivingstone1997415-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He is considered a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, various <a href="/wiki/Lutheran" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran">Lutheran</a> denominations, and other <a href="/wiki/Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant">Protestant</a> denominations. Immediately after his death, Gregory was canonized by popular acclaim.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005710_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005710-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The Protestant reformer <a href="/wiki/John_Calvin" title="John Calvin">John Calvin</a> admired Gregory greatly and declared in his <a href="/wiki/Institutes_of_the_Christian_Religion" title="Institutes of the Christian Religion"><i>Institutes</i></a> that Gregory was the last good pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalvin1845125Bk_IV,_Ch._7_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalvin1845125Bk_IV,_Ch._7-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELittle1963145–157_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELittle1963145–157-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of musicians, singers, students, and teachers.<sup id="cite_ref-scborromeo.org_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scborromeo.org-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Gregory was born around 540<sup id="cite_ref-Totila_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Totila-12">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> in Rome, then recently <a href="/wiki/Gothic_War_(535-554)" class="mw-redirect" title="Gothic War (535-554)">reconquered</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Roman_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Roman Empire">Eastern Roman Empire</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Ostrogothic_Kingdom" title="Ostrogothic Kingdom">Ostrogoths</a>. His parents named him <i>Gregorius</i>, which according to <a href="/wiki/%C3%86lfric_of_Eynsham" title="Ælfric of Eynsham">Ælfric of Eynsham</a> in <i>An Homily on the Birth-Day of S. Gregory,</i> "... is a Greek Name, which signifies in the Latin Tongue, <i>Vigilantius</i>, that is in English, Watchful..."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEÆlfric17094_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEÆlfric17094-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The medieval writer who provided this etymology<sup id="cite_ref-translator_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-translator-14">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> did not hesitate to apply it to the life of Gregory. Ælfric states, "He was very diligent in God's Commandments."<sup id="cite_ref-grēgorein_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grēgorein-15">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory was born into a wealthy noble Roman family with close connections to the church. His father, Gordianus, a patrician<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> who served as a senator and for a time was the Prefect of the City of Rome,<sup id="cite_ref-Thornton_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thornton-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> also held the position of <a href="/wiki/Regionarius" title="Regionarius">Regionarius</a> in the church, though nothing further is known about that position. Gregory's mother, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Silvia" title="Saint Silvia">Silvia</a>, was well-born, and had a married sister, Pateria, in <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a>. His mother and two paternal aunts are honored by Catholic and Orthodox churches as saints.<sup id="cite_ref-Thornton_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thornton-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-OCA_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCA-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's great-great-grandfather had been <a href="/wiki/Pope_Felix_III" title="Pope Felix III">Pope Felix III</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-IIIorIV_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IIIorIV-17">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> the nominee of the Gothic king, <a href="/wiki/Theodoric_the_Great" title="Theodoric the Great">Theodoric</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Dud4_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dud4-18">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's election to the throne of St. Peter made his family the most distinguished clerical dynasty of the period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards1980_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards1980-19">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The family owned and resided in a <i><a href="/wiki/Villa_suburbana" class="mw-redirect" title="Villa suburbana">villa suburbana</a></i> on the <a href="/wiki/Caelian_Hill" title="Caelian Hill">Caelian Hill</a>, fronting the same street (now the Via di San Gregorio) as the former palaces of the Roman emperors on the <a href="/wiki/Palatine_Hill" title="Palatine Hill">Palatine Hill</a> opposite. The north of the street runs into the <a href="/wiki/Colosseum" title="Colosseum">Colosseum</a>; the south, the <a href="/wiki/Circus_Maximus" title="Circus Maximus">Circus Maximus</a>. In Gregory's day the ancient buildings were in ruins and were privately owned.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Villas covered the area. Gregory's family also owned working estates in <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> and around Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards198025_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards198025-22">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Gregory later had portraits done in fresco in their former home on the Caelian and these were described 300 years later by <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Hymonides" title="Johannes Hymonides">John the Deacon</a>. Gordianus was tall with a long face and light eyes. He wore a beard. <a href="/wiki/Saint_Silvia" title="Saint Silvia">Silvia</a> was tall, had a round face, blue eyes and a cheerful look. They had another son whose name and fate are unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory was born into a period of upheaval in Italy. From 542 the so-called <a href="/wiki/Plague_of_Justinian" title="Plague of Justinian">Plague of Justinian</a> swept through the provinces of the empire, including Italy. The plague caused famine, panic, and sometimes rioting. In some parts of the country, over a third of the population was wiped out or destroyed, with heavy spiritual and emotional effects on the people of the empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus19974–5_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus19974–5-24">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Politically, although the <a href="/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire" title="Western Roman Empire">Western Roman Empire</a> had long since vanished in favor of the Gothic kings of Italy, during the 540s <a href="/wiki/Italy_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="Italy in the Middle Ages">Italy</a> was gradually retaken from the <a href="/wiki/Goths" title="Goths">Goths</a> by <a href="/wiki/Justinian_I" title="Justinian I">Justinian I</a>, emperor of the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Eastern Roman Empire</a> ruling from <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>. As the fighting was mainly in the north, the young Gregory probably saw little of it. <a href="/wiki/Totila" title="Totila">Totila</a> <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(546)" title="Sack of Rome (546)">sacked and vacated Rome</a> in 546, destroying most of its population, but in 549 he invited those who were still alive to return to the empty and ruined streets. It has been hypothesized that young Gregory and his parents retired during that intermission to their Sicilian estates, to return in 549.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Gothic_War_(535%E2%80%93554)" title="Gothic War (535–554)">war</a> was over in Rome by 552, and a subsequent invasion of the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a> was defeated in 554. After that, there was peace in Italy, and the appearance of restoration, except that the central government now resided in Constantinople.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Like most young men of his position in Roman society, Gregory was well educated, learning grammar, rhetoric, the sciences, literature, and law; he excelled in all these fields.<sup id="cite_ref-Thornton_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thornton-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Tours" title="Gregory of Tours">Gregory of Tours</a> reported that "in grammar, dialectic and rhetoric ... he was second to none...."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards198026_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards198026-26">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He wrote correct Latin but did not read or write Greek. He knew Latin authors, natural science, history, mathematics and music and had such a "fluency with imperial law" that he may have trained in it "as a preparation for a career in public life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards198026_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards198026-26">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, he became a government official, advancing quickly in rank to become, like his father, Prefect of Rome, the highest civil office in the city, when only thirty-three years old.<sup id="cite_ref-Thornton_16-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thornton-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The monks of the <a href="/wiki/Monastery_of_Saint_Andrew" class="mw-redirect" title="Monastery of Saint Andrew">Monastery of St. Andrew</a>, established by Gregory at the ancestral home on the Caelian, had a portrait of him made after his death, which John the Deacon also saw in the 9th century. He reports the picture of a man who was "rather bald" and had a "tawny" beard like his father's and a face that was intermediate in shape between his mother's and father's. The hair that he had on the sides was long and carefully curled. His nose was "thin and straight" and "slightly aquiline". "His forehead was high." He had thick, "subdivided" lips and a chin "of a comely prominence" and "beautiful hands".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards198044_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards198044-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the modern era, Gregory is often depicted as a man at the border, poised between the Roman and Germanic worlds, between East and West, and above all, perhaps, between the ancient and medieval epochs.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Monastic_years">Monastic years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Monastic years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On his father's death, Gregory converted his family <i>villa</i> into a <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monastery</a> dedicated to <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew the Apostle</a> (after his death it was rededicated as <a href="/wiki/San_Gregorio_Magno_al_Celio" title="San Gregorio Magno al Celio">San Gregorio Magno al Celio</a>). In his life of contemplation, Gregory concluded that "in that silence of the heart, while we keep watch within through contemplation, we are as if asleep to all things that are without."<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory had a deep respect for the monastic life and particularly the vow of poverty. Thus, when it came to light that a monk lying on his death bed had stolen three gold pieces, Gregory, as a remedial punishment, forced the monk to die alone, then threw his body and coins on a manure heap to rot with a condemnation, "Take your money with you to perdition." Gregory believed that punishment of sins can begin, even in this life before death.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> However, in time, after the monk's death, Gregory had 30 Masses offered for the man to <a href="/wiki/Prayer_for_the_dead" title="Prayer for the dead">assist his soul</a> before the <a href="/wiki/Final_judgment" class="mw-redirect" title="Final judgment">final judgment</a>. He viewed being a monk as the "ardent quest for the vision of our Creator."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus199769_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus199769-31">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> His three paternal aunts were nuns renowned for their sanctity. However, after the eldest two, <a href="/wiki/Trasilla_and_Emiliana" title="Trasilla and Emiliana">Trasilla and Emiliana</a>, died after seeing a vision of their ancestor <a href="/wiki/Pope_Felix_III" title="Pope Felix III">Pope Felix III</a>, the youngest soon abandoned the religious life and married the steward of her estate. Gregory's response to this family scandal was that "many are called but few are chosen."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards198026_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards198026-26">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's mother, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Silvia" title="Saint Silvia">Silvia</a>, is herself a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a>.
</p><p>Eventually, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pelagius_II" title="Pope Pelagius II">Pope Pelagius II</a> ordained Gregory a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> and solicited his help in trying to heal the <a href="/wiki/Schism_of_the_Three_Chapters" title="Schism of the Three Chapters">schism of the Three Chapters</a> in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Italy" title="Northern Italy">northern Italy</a>. However, this schism was not healed until well after Gregory was gone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus19973_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus19973-32">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Apocrisiariate_.28579.E2.80.93585.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Apocrisiariate_(579–585)">Apocrisiariate (579–585)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Apocrisiariate (579–585)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg/170px-Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg/255px-Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg/340px-Moralia_in_Job_MS_dragonslayer.jpg 2x" data-file-width="669" data-file-height="914" /></a><figcaption>Illumination in a 12th-century <a href="/wiki/Manuscript" title="Manuscript">manuscript</a> of a letter of Gregory's to <a href="/wiki/Leander_of_Seville" title="Leander of Seville">Leander</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Seville" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Seville">bishop of Seville</a> (Bibl. Municipale, MS 2, <a href="/wiki/Dijon" title="Dijon">Dijon</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 579, Pelagius II chose Gregory as his <i><a href="/wiki/Apocrisiarius" title="Apocrisiarius">apocrisiarius</a></i> (ambassador to the imperial court in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>), a post Gregory would hold until 586.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Gregory was part of the Roman delegation (both lay and clerical) that arrived in Constantinople in 578 to ask the emperor for military aid against the <a href="/wiki/Lombards" title="Lombards">Lombards</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-e9_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e9-34">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> With the Byzantine military <a href="/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sassanid_War_of_572%E2%80%93591" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine–Sassanid War of 572–591">focused on the East</a>, these entreaties proved unsuccessful; in 584, Pelagius II wrote to Gregory as <i>apocrisiarius</i>, detailing the hardships that Rome was experiencing under the Lombards and asking him to ask <a href="/wiki/Emperor_Maurice" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Maurice">Emperor Maurice</a> to send a relief force.<sup id="cite_ref-e9_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e9-34">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Maurice, however, had long ago determined to limit his efforts against the Lombards to intrigue and diplomacy, pitting the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a> against them.<sup id="cite_ref-e9_34-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e9-34">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> It soon became obvious to Gregory that the Byzantine emperors were unlikely to send such a force, given their more immediate difficulties with the Persians in the East and the <a href="/wiki/Pannonian_Avars" title="Pannonian Avars">Avars</a> and <a href="/wiki/Slavs" title="Slavs">Slavs</a> to the North.<sup id="cite_ref-e10_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e10-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Ekonomou, "if Gregory's principal task was to plead Rome's cause before the emperor, there seems to have been little left for him to do once imperial policy toward Italy became evident. Papal representatives who pressed their claims with excessive vigor could quickly become a nuisance and find themselves excluded from the imperial presence altogether".<sup id="cite_ref-e10_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e10-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Gregory had already drawn an imperial rebuke for his lengthy canonical writings on the subject of the legitimacy of <a href="/wiki/John_III_Scholasticus" class="mw-redirect" title="John III Scholasticus">John III Scholasticus</a>, who had occupied the Patriarchate of Constantinople for twelve years prior to the return of <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Eutychius_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Eutychius of Constantinople">Eutychius</a> (who had been driven out by Justinian).<sup id="cite_ref-e10_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e10-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Gregory turned to cultivating connections with the Byzantine elite of the city, where he became extremely popular with the city's upper class, "especially aristocratic women".<sup id="cite_ref-e10_35-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e10-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Ekonomou surmises that "while Gregory may have become spiritual father to a large and important segment of Constantinople's aristocracy, this relationship did not significantly advance the interests of Rome before the emperor".<sup id="cite_ref-e10_35-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e10-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Although the writings of John the Deacon claim that Gregory "labored diligently for the relief of Italy", there is no evidence that his tenure accomplished much towards any of the objectives of Pelagius II.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory's theological disputes with Patriarch Eutychius would leave a "bitter taste for the theological speculation of the East" with Gregory that continued to influence him well into his own papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-e11_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e11-37">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> According to Western sources, Gregory's very public debate with Eutychius culminated in an exchange before <a href="/wiki/Tiberius_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Tiberius II">Tiberius II</a> where Gregory cited a biblical passage<sup id="cite_ref-Palpate_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Palpate-38">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> in support of the view that Christ was corporeal and palpable after his Resurrection; allegedly as a result of this exchange, Tiberius II ordered Eutychius's writings burned.<sup id="cite_ref-e11_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e11-37">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Ekonomou views this argument, though exaggerated in Western sources, as Gregory's "one achievement of an otherwise fruitless <i>apokrisiariat</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-e12_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e12-39">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In reality, Gregory was forced to rely on Scripture because he could not read the untranslated Greek authoritative works.<sup id="cite_ref-e12_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e12-39">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory left Constantinople for Rome in 585, returning to his monastery on the <a href="/wiki/Caelian_Hill" title="Caelian Hill">Caelian Hill</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-e13_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e13-40">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Gregory was elected by <a href="/wiki/Acclamation" title="Acclamation">acclamation</a> to succeed Pelagius II in 590, when the latter died of the <a href="/wiki/Plague_(disease)" title="Plague (disease)">plague</a> spreading through the city.<sup id="cite_ref-e13_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e13-40">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Gregory was approved by an Imperial <i><a href="/wiki/Iussio" class="mw-redirect" title="Iussio">iussio</a></i> from Constantinople the following September (as was the norm during the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Papacy" title="Byzantine Papacy">Byzantine Papacy</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-e13_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-e13-40">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Controversy_with_Eutychius">Controversy with Eutychius</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Controversy with Eutychius">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In Constantinople, Gregory took issue with the aged <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Eutychius_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Eutychius of Constantinople">Patriarch Eutychius of Constantinople</a>, who had recently published a treatise, now lost, on the <a href="/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_dead" class="mw-redirect" title="Resurrection of the dead">General Resurrection</a>. Eutychius maintained that the resurrected body "will be more subtle than air, and no longer palpable".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmithWace1880415_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmithWace1880415-41">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> Gregory opposed with the palpability of the risen Christ in Luke 24:39.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> As the dispute could not be settled, the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine emperor">Byzantine emperor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tiberius_II_Constantine" title="Tiberius II Constantine">Tiberius II Constantine</a>, undertook to arbitrate. He decided in favor of palpability and ordered Eutychius' book to be <a href="/wiki/Book_burning" title="Book burning">burned</a>. Shortly after both Gregory and Eutychius became ill; Gregory recovered, but Eutychius died on 5 April 582, at age 70. On his deathbed Eutychius recanted impalpability and Gregory dropped the matter.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy">Papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg/220px-Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="246" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg/330px-Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg/440px-Pictures_of_English_History_Plate_V_-_Saint_Augustine_and_the_Saxons.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1196" data-file-height="1336" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Canterbury" title="Augustine of Canterbury">Augustine of Canterbury</a> on <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_mission" title="Gregorian mission">his mission</a> to <a href="/wiki/Anglo_Saxon_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo Saxon England">Anglo-Saxon England</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Gregory was more inclined to remain retired into the monastic lifestyle of contemplation.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In texts of all genres, especially those produced in his first year as pope, Gregory bemoaned the burden of office and mourned the loss of the undisturbed life of
prayer he had once enjoyed as a monk.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
When he became pope in 590, among his first acts was writing a series of letters disavowing any ambition to the throne of Peter and praising the contemplative life of the monks. At that time, for various reasons, the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a> had not exerted effective leadership in the West since the pontificate of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gelasius_I" title="Pope Gelasius I">Gelasius I</a>. The episcopacy in <a href="/wiki/Gaul" title="Gaul">Gaul</a> was drawn from the great territorial families, and identified with them: the parochial horizon of Gregory's contemporary, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Tours" title="Gregory of Tours">Gregory of Tours</a>, may be considered typical; in <a href="/wiki/Visigoths" title="Visigoths">Visigothic</a> Spain the bishops had little contact with Rome; in Italy the territories which had <i>de facto</i> fallen under the administration of the papacy were beset by the violent <a href="/wiki/Lombards" title="Lombards">Lombard</a> dukes and the rivalry of the Byzantines in the <a href="/wiki/Exarchate_of_Ravenna" title="Exarchate of Ravenna">Exarchate of Ravenna</a> and in the south.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Pope Gregory had strong convictions on missions: "Almighty God places good men in authority that He may impart through them the gifts of His mercy to their subjects. And this we find to be the case with the British over whom you have been appointed to rule, that through the blessings bestowed on you the blessings of heaven might be bestowed on your people also."<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> He is credited with re-energizing the Church's missionary work among the non-Christian peoples of northern Europe. He is most famous for sending a mission, often called the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_mission" title="Gregorian mission">Gregorian mission</a>, under <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Canterbury" title="Augustine of Canterbury">Augustine of Canterbury</a>, prior of Saint Andrew's, where he had perhaps succeeded Gregory, to evangelize the pagan <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxons" title="Anglo-Saxons">Anglo-Saxons</a> of England. It seems that the pope had never forgotten the English slaves whom he had once seen in the Roman Forum.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The mission was successful, and it was from England that missionaries later set out for the Netherlands and Germany. The preaching of non-heretical Christian faith and the
elimination of all deviations from it was a key element in Gregory's worldview, and it constituted one of the major continuing policies of his pontificate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards1980228_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards1980228-48">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Pope Gregory the Great urged his followers on the <a href="/wiki/Hygiene_in_Christianity" title="Hygiene in Christianity">value of bathing</a> as a bodily need.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESquatriti200254_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESquatriti200254-49">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is said he was <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">declared a saint</a> immediately after his death by "popular acclamation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his official documents, Gregory was the first to make extensive use of the term "<a href="/wiki/Servant_of_the_Servants_of_God" class="mw-redirect" title="Servant of the Servants of God">Servant of the Servants of God</a>" (<i>servus servorum Dei</i>) as a papal title, thus initiating a practice that was to be followed by most subsequent popes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeehan1912_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeehan1912-50">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Alms">Alms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Alms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Church had a practice from early times of passing on a large portion of the donations it received from its members as <a href="/wiki/Alms" title="Alms">alms</a>. As pope, Gregory did his utmost to encourage that high standard among church personnel.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Gregory is known for his extensive administrative system of charitable relief of the poor at Rome. The poor were predominantly refugees from the incursions of the <a href="/wiki/Lombards" title="Lombards">Lombards</a>. The philosophy under which he devised this system is that the wealth belonged to the poor and the church was only its steward. He received lavish donations from the wealthy families of Rome, who, following his own example, were eager, by doing so, to expiate their sins. He gave alms equally as lavishly both individually and en masse. He wrote in letters:<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> "I have frequently charged you ... to act as my representative ... to relieve the poor in their distress ...." and "... I hold the office of steward to the property of the poor ...."
</p><p>In Gregory's time, the Church in Rome received donations of many different kinds: <a href="/wiki/Consumables" title="Consumables">consumables</a> such as food and clothing; investment property: real estate and works of art; and <a href="/wiki/Capital_goods" class="mw-redirect" title="Capital goods">capital goods</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Revenue" title="Revenue">revenue</a>-generating property, such as the <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicilian</a> <a href="/wiki/Latifundia" class="mw-redirect" title="Latifundia">latifundia</a>, or agricultural estates. The Church already had a system for circulating the consumables to the poor: associated with each of the main city churches was a <i>diaconium</i> or office of the <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a>. He was given a building from which the poor could apply for assistance at any time.<sup id="cite_ref-Later_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Later-52">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmithCheetham1875549_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmithCheetham1875549-53">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The circumstances in which Gregory became pope in 590 were of ruination. The Lombards held the greater part of Italy. Their depredations had brought the economy to a standstill. They camped nearly at the gates of Rome. The city itself was crowded with refugees from all walks of life, who lived in the streets and had few of the necessities of life. The seat of government was far from Rome in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> and appeared unable to undertake the relief of Italy. The pope had sent emissaries, including Gregory, asking for assistance, to no avail.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mass_of_St._Gregory,_c._1490,_attributed_to_Diego_de_la_Cruz,_oil_and_gold_on_panel_(Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/67/Mass_of_St._Gregory%2C_c._1490%2C_attributed_to_Diego_de_la_Cruz%2C_oil_and_gold_on_panel_%28Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%29.jpg/170px-Mass_of_St._Gregory%2C_c._1490%2C_attributed_to_Diego_de_la_Cruz%2C_oil_and_gold_on_panel_%28Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/67/Mass_of_St._Gregory%2C_c._1490%2C_attributed_to_Diego_de_la_Cruz%2C_oil_and_gold_on_panel_%28Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%29.jpg/255px-Mass_of_St._Gregory%2C_c._1490%2C_attributed_to_Diego_de_la_Cruz%2C_oil_and_gold_on_panel_%28Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/67/Mass_of_St._Gregory%2C_c._1490%2C_attributed_to_Diego_de_la_Cruz%2C_oil_and_gold_on_panel_%28Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="280" data-file-height="378" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Mass_of_St._Gregory" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass of St. Gregory">Mass of St. Gregory</a></i>, <span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1490</span>, attributed to Diego de la Cruz, oil and gold on panel (<a href="/wiki/Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art" title="Philadelphia Museum of Art">Philadelphia Museum of Art</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 590, Gregory could wait for Constantinople no longer. He organized the resources of the church into an administration for general relief. In doing so he evidenced a talent for and intuitive understanding of the principles of accounting, which was not to be invented for centuries. The church already had basic accounting documents: every <a href="/wiki/Expense" title="Expense">expense</a> was recorded in <a href="/wiki/Double-entry_bookkeeping_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Double-entry bookkeeping system">journals</a> called <i>regesta</i>, "lists" of amounts, recipients and circumstances. Revenue was recorded in <i>polyptici</i>, "<a href="/wiki/Bookkeeping" title="Bookkeeping">books</a>". Many of these polyptici were <a href="/wiki/Ledger" title="Ledger">ledgers</a> recording the operating expenses of the church and the <a href="/wiki/Asset" title="Asset">assets</a>, the <i>patrimonia</i>. A central papal administration, the <i>notarii</i>, under a chief, the <i>primicerius notariorum</i>, kept the ledgers and issued <i>brevia patrimonii</i>, or lists of property for which each <i><a href="/wiki/Rector_(ecclesiastical)" title="Rector (ecclesiastical)">rector</a></i> was responsible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMann1914322_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMann1914322-54">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory began by aggressively requiring his churchmen to seek out and relieve needy persons and reprimanded them if they did not. In a letter to a subordinate in <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a> he wrote: "I asked you most of all to take care of the poor. And if you knew of people in poverty, you should have pointed them out ... I desire that you give the woman, Pateria, forty <a href="/wiki/Solidus_(coin)" title="Solidus (coin)">solidi</a> for the children's shoes and forty bushels of grain ...."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmbrosiniWillis199666–67_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmbrosiniWillis199666–67-55">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Soon he was replacing administrators who would not cooperate with those who would and at the same time adding more in a build-up to a great plan that he had in mind. He understood that expenses must be matched by income. To pay for his increased expenses he liquidated the investment property and paid the expenses in cash according to a budget recorded in the polyptici. The churchmen were paid four times a year and also personally given a golden coin for their trouble.<sup id="cite_ref-dud248_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dud248-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Money, however, was no substitute for food in a city that was on the brink of famine. Even the wealthy were going hungry in their villas.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The church now owned between 1,300 and 1,800 square miles (3,400 and 4,700&#160;km<sup>2</sup>) of revenue-generating farmland divided into large sections called <i>patrimonia.</i> It produced goods of all kinds, which were sold, but Gregory intervened and had the goods shipped to Rome for distribution in the <i>diaconia</i>. He gave orders to step up production, set quotas and put an administrative structure in place to carry it out. At the bottom was the <i>rusticus</i> who produced the goods. Some rustici were or owned slaves. He turned over part of his produce to a <i>conductor</i> from whom he leased the land. The latter reported to an <i>actionarius</i>, the latter to a <i>defensor</i> and the latter to a <i>rector</i>. Grain, wine, cheese, meat, fish and oil began to arrive at Rome in large quantities, where it was given away for nothing as alms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeanesly196922–24_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeanesly196922–24-57">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Distributions to qualified persons were monthly. However, a certain proportion of the population lived in the streets or were too ill or infirm to pick up their monthly food supply. To them Gregory sent out a small army of charitable persons, mainly monks, every morning with prepared food. It is said that he would not dine until the indigent were fed. When he did dine he shared the family table, which he had saved (and which still exists), with 12 indigent guests. To the needy living in wealthy homes he sent meals he had cooked with his own hands as gifts to spare them the indignity of receiving charity. Hearing of the death of an indigent in a back room he was depressed for days, entertaining for a time the conceit that he had failed in his duty and was a murderer.<sup id="cite_ref-dud248_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dud248-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>These and other good deeds and charitable frame of mind completely won the hearts and minds of the Roman people. They now looked to the papacy for government, ignoring the state at Constantinople. The office of urban prefect went without candidates. From the time of Gregory the Great to the rise of <a href="/wiki/Italian_nationalism" title="Italian nationalism">Italian nationalism</a> the papacy was the most influential presence in Italy.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Liturgical_reforms">Liturgical reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Liturgical reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John the Deacon wrote that Pope Gregory I made a general revision of the liturgy of the <a href="/wiki/Pre-Tridentine_Mass" title="Pre-Tridentine Mass">Pre-Tridentine Mass</a>, "removing many things, changing a few, adding some". In his own letters, Gregory remarks that he moved the <i><a href="/wiki/Pater_Noster" class="mw-redirect" title="Pater Noster">Pater Noster</a></i> (Our Father) to immediately after the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Canon" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Canon">Roman Canon</a> and immediately before the <a href="/wiki/Fraction_(religion)" title="Fraction (religion)">Fraction</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEden2004487_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2004487-58">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> This position is still maintained today in the Roman Liturgy. The pre-Gregorian position is evident in the <a href="/wiki/Ambrosian_Rite" title="Ambrosian Rite">Ambrosian Rite</a>. Gregory added material to the <i><a href="/wiki/Roman_Canon#Hanc_Igitur" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Canon">Hanc Igitur</a></i> of the Roman Canon and established the nine <i><a href="/wiki/Kyrie" title="Kyrie">Kyries</a></i> (a vestigial remnant of the <a href="/wiki/Litany" title="Litany">litany</a> which was originally at that place) at the beginning of <a href="/wiki/Mass_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass (Catholic Church)">Mass</a>. He also reduced the role of deacons in the Roman Liturgy.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Sacramentary" title="Sacramentary">Sacramentaries</a> directly influenced by Gregorian reforms are referred to as <i>Sacrementaria Gregoriana</i>. Roman and other <a href="/wiki/Latin_liturgical_rites" title="Latin liturgical rites">Western liturgies</a> since this era have a number of prayers that change to reflect the feast or liturgical season; these variations are visible in the <a href="/wiki/Collect" title="Collect">collects</a> and <a href="/wiki/Preface_(liturgy)" title="Preface (liturgy)">prefaces</a> as well as in the Roman Canon itself.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Divine_Liturgy_of_the_Presanctified_Gifts">Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a>, Gregory is credited as the primary influence in constructing the more penitential <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy_of_the_Presanctified_Gifts" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts">Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts</a>, a fully separate form of the <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy" title="Divine Liturgy">Divine Liturgy</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> adapted to the needs of the season of <a href="/wiki/Great_Lent" title="Great Lent">Great Lent</a>. Its <a href="/wiki/Roman_Rite" title="Roman Rite">Roman Rite</a> equivalent is the <a href="/wiki/Mass_of_the_Presanctified" title="Mass of the Presanctified">Mass of the Presanctified</a> used only on <a href="/wiki/Good_Friday" title="Good Friday">Good Friday</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Christianity" title="Syriac Christianity">Syriac</a> Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts continues to be used in the <a href="/wiki/Malankara_Rite" title="Malankara Rite">Malankara Rite</a>, a variant of the <a href="/wiki/West_Syrian_Rite" class="mw-redirect" title="West Syrian Rite">West Syrian Rite</a> historically practiced in the <a href="/wiki/Malankara_Church" title="Malankara Church">Malankara Church</a> of <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a>, and now practiced by the several churches that descended from it and at some occasions in the <a href="/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East" title="Assyrian Church of the East">Assyrian Church of the East</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChupungco199717_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChupungco199717-59">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Gregorian_chant">Gregorian chant</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Gregorian chant">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg/170px-Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="206" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg/255px-Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg/340px-Gregory_I_-_Antiphonary_of_Hartker_of_Sankt_Gallen.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5272" data-file-height="6376" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Antiphonary" title="Antiphonary">Antiphonary</a> of Hartker of the monastery of Saint Gall</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_chant" title="Gregorian chant">Gregorian chant</a></div>
<p>The mainstream form of Western <a href="/wiki/Plainchant" class="mw-redirect" title="Plainchant">plainchant</a>, standardized in the late 9th century,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELevy19987_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELevy19987-60">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> was attributed to Pope Gregory I and so took the name of Gregorian chant. The earliest such attribution is in John the Deacon's 873 biography of Gregory, almost three centuries after the pope's death, and the chant that bears his name "is the result of the fusion of Roman and Frankish elements which took place in the Franco-German empire under <a href="/wiki/Pepin_the_Short" title="Pepin the Short">Pepin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a> and their successors".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurray19633–4_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurray19633–4-61">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Window,_Gregory_the_Great,_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Window%2C_Gregory_the_Great%2C_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg/170px-Window%2C_Gregory_the_Great%2C_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="340" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Window%2C_Gregory_the_Great%2C_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg/255px-Window%2C_Gregory_the_Great%2C_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Window%2C_Gregory_the_Great%2C_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg/340px-Window%2C_Gregory_the_Great%2C_Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd.jpg 2x" data-file-width="973" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption>St. Gregory the Great window, Episcopal <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Good_Shepherd_(Rosemont,_Pennsylvania)" title="Church of the Good Shepherd (Rosemont, Pennsylvania)">Church of the Good Shepherd (Rosemont, Pennsylvania)</a> US</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gregory is commonly credited with founding the medieval papacy and so many attribute the beginning of medieval spirituality to him.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
Gregory is the only pope between the fifth and the eleventh centuries whose correspondence and writings have survived enough to form a comprehensive <i>corpus</i>. Some of his writings are:
</p>
<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/Moralia_in_Job" title="Moralia in Job">Moralia in Job</a></i>. This is one of the longest patristic works. It was possibly finished as early as 591. It is based on talks Gregory gave on the Book of Job to his 'brethren' who accompanied him to Constantinople. The work as we have it is the result of Gregory's revision and completion of it soon after his accession to the papal office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus199715_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus199715-63">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Pastoral_Care" title="Pastoral Care">Pastoral Care</a></i> (<i>Liber regulae pastoralis</i>), in which he contrasted the role of bishops as pastors of their flock with their position as nobles of the church: the definitive statement of the nature of the episcopal office. This was probably begun before his election as pope and finished in 591.</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Dialogues_(Pope_Gregory)" class="mw-redirect" title="Dialogues (Pope Gregory)">Dialogues</a></i>, a collection of four books of miracles, signs, wonders, and healings done by the holy men, mostly monastic, of sixth-century Italy, with the second book entirely devoted to a popular life of <a href="/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia" title="Benedict of Nursia">Saint Benedict</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGardner1911_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGardner1911-64">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sermons" class="mw-redirect" title="Sermons">Sermons</a>, including:
<ul><li>The sermons include the 22 <i>Homilae in Hiezechielem </i>(<i>Homilies on Ezekiel</i>), dealing with Ezekiel 1.1–4.3 in Book One, and Ezekiel 40 in Book 2. These were preached during 592–3, the years that the Lombards besieged Rome, and contain some of Gregory's most profound mystical teachings. They were revised eight years later.</li>
<li>The <i>Homilae xl in Evangelia </i>(<i>Forty Homilies on the Gospels</i>) for the liturgical year, delivered during 591 and 592, which were seemingly finished by 593. A papyrus fragment from this codex survives in the <a href="/wiki/British_Museum" title="British Museum">British Museum</a>, London, UK.<sup id="cite_ref-britishlibrary_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britishlibrary-65">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Expositio in Canticis Canticorum. </i>Only 2 of these sermons on the Song of Songs survive, discussing the text up to Song 1.9.</li></ul></li>
<li><i>In Librum primum regum expositio </i>(<i>Commentary on 1 Kings</i>), which scholars now think that this is a work by 12th century monk, <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Cava" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter of Cava">Peter of Cava</a>, who used no longer extant Gregorian material.</li>
<li>Copies of some 854 letters have survived. During Gregory's time, copies of papal letters were made by scribes into a <i>Registrum </i>(<i>Register</i>), which was then kept in the <i>scrinium</i>. It is known that in the 9th century, when John the Deacon composed his <i>Life</i> of Gregory, the <i>Registrum</i> of Gregory's letters was formed of 14 papyrus rolls (though it is difficult to estimate how many letters this may have represented). Though these original rolls are now lost, the 854 letters have survived in copies made at various later times, the largest single batch of 686 letters being made by order of <a href="/wiki/Adrian_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Adrian I">Adrian I</a> (772–95).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus199715_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus199715-63">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The majority of the copies, dating from the 10th to the 15th century, are stored in the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Library" title="Vatican Library">Vatican Library</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmbrosiniWillis199663–64_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmbrosiniWillis199663–64-66">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Gregory wrote over 850 letters in the last 13 years of his life (590–604) that give us an accurate picture of his work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus1997i_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus1997i-67">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> A truly autobiographical presentation is nearly impossible for Gregory. The development of his mind and personality remains purely speculative in nature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus19972_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus19972-68">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Opinions of the writings of Gregory vary. "His character strikes us as an ambiguous and enigmatic one," the Jewish Canadian-American popularist <a href="/wiki/Norman_F._Cantor" class="mw-redirect" title="Norman F. Cantor">Cantor</a> observed. "On the one hand he was an able and determined administrator, a skilled and clever diplomat, a leader of the greatest sophistication and vision; but on the other hand, he appears in his writings as a superstitious and credulous <a href="/wiki/Monk" title="Monk">monk</a>, hostile to learning, crudely limited as a theologian, and excessively devoted to saints, <a href="/wiki/Miracle" title="Miracle">miracles</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Identification_of_three_figures_in_the_Gospels">Identification of three figures in the Gospels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Identification of three figures in the Gospels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gregory was among those who identified <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a> with <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Bethany" title="Mary of Bethany">Mary of Bethany</a>, whom John 12:1-8 recounts as having <a href="/wiki/Anointing_of_Jesus" title="Anointing of Jesus">anointed Jesus</a> with precious ointment,<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> an event that some interpret as being the same as the <a href="/wiki/Parable_of_the_Two_Debtors" title="Parable of the Two Debtors">anointing of Jesus</a> performed by a woman that Luke (alone among the <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">synoptic Gospels</a>) recounts as a sinner.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Preaching on the passage in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a>, Gregory remarked: "This woman, whom Luke calls a sinner<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> and John calls Mary,<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> I think is the Mary from whom Mark reports<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> that seven demons were cast out."<sup id="cite_ref-HancVero_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HancVero-75">&#91;i&#93;</a></sup> Modern biblical scholars<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (February 2021)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> distinguish these as three separate figures/persons, but within the general populace (and even some <a href="/wiki/Theological_seminary" class="mw-redirect" title="Theological seminary">seminaries</a>),<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (February 2021)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> they are still believed to refer to the same person.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch1998156Ch.10_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch1998156Ch.10-76">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Iconography">Iconography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Iconography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gregorythegreat.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Gregorythegreat.jpg/170px-Gregorythegreat.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Gregorythegreat.jpg/255px-Gregorythegreat.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Gregorythegreat.jpg/340px-Gregorythegreat.jpg 2x" data-file-width="770" data-file-height="1080" /></a><figcaption><i>Saint Gregory the Great</i> by <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_Ribera" class="mw-redirect" title="José de Ribera">José de Ribera</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In art Gregory is usually shown in full pontifical robes with the <a href="/wiki/Papal_tiara" title="Papal tiara">tiara</a> and <a href="/wiki/Patriarchal_cross" title="Patriarchal cross">double cross</a>, despite his actual habit of dress. Earlier depictions are more likely to show a monastic <a href="/wiki/Tonsure" title="Tonsure">tonsure</a> and plainer dress. Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Icons" class="mw-redirect" title="Icons">icons</a> traditionally show St. Gregory vested as a bishop holding a <a href="/wiki/Gospel_Book" title="Gospel Book">Gospel Book</a> and blessing with his right hand. It is recorded that he permitted his depiction with a <a href="/wiki/Square_halo" class="mw-redirect" title="Square halo">square halo</a>, then used for the living.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGietmann1911_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGietmann1911-77">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> A dove is his <a href="/wiki/Emblem" title="Emblem">attribute</a>, from the well-known story attributed to his friend Peter the Deacon,<sup id="cite_ref-Colgrave_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Colgrave-78">&#91;j&#93;</a></sup> who tells that when the pope was dictating his homilies on <a href="/wiki/Ezechiel" class="mw-redirect" title="Ezechiel">Ezechiel</a> a curtain was drawn between his secretary and himself. As, however, the pope remained silent for long periods at a time, the servant made a hole in the curtain and, looking through, beheld a dove seated upon Gregory's head with its beak between his lips. When the dove withdrew its beak the pope spoke and the secretary took down his words; but when he became silent the servant again applied his eye to the hole and saw the dove had replaced its beak between his lips.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuddleston1909-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ribera's oil painting of <i>Saint Gregory the Great</i> (<span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1614</span>) is from the Giustiniani collection. The painting is conserved in the <a href="/wiki/Galleria_Nazionale_d%27Arte_Antica" title="Galleria Nazionale d&#39;Arte Antica">Galleria Nazionale d'Arte Antica</a>, Rome. The face of Gregory is a caricature of the features described by John the Deacon: total baldness, outthrust chin, beak-like nose, whereas John had described partial baldness, a mildly protruding chin, slightly aquiline nose and strikingly good looks. In this picture also Gregory has his monastic back on the world, which the real Gregory, despite his reclusive intent, was seldom allowed to have.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2017)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>This scene is shown as a version of the traditional <a href="/wiki/Evangelist_portrait" title="Evangelist portrait">Evangelist portrait</a> (where the <a href="/wiki/Evangelist_symbols" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelist symbols">Evangelists' symbols</a> are also sometimes shown dictating) from the tenth century onwards. An early example is the <a href="/wiki/Dedication_miniature" class="mw-redirect" title="Dedication miniature">dedication miniature</a> from an eleventh-century manuscript of Gregory's <i>Moralia in Job</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The miniature shows the scribe, Bebo of <a href="/wiki/Seeon_Abbey" title="Seeon Abbey">Seeon Abbey</a>, presenting the manuscript to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Henry II, Holy Roman Emperor">Henry II</a>. In the upper left the author is seen writing the text under divine inspiration. Usually the dove is shown whispering in Gregory's ear for a clearer composition.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_(1440)_by_Robert_Campin.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_%281440%29_by_Robert_Campin.jpg/170px-Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_%281440%29_by_Robert_Campin.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="206" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_%281440%29_by_Robert_Campin.jpg/255px-Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_%281440%29_by_Robert_Campin.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_%281440%29_by_Robert_Campin.jpg/340px-Mass_of_Saint_Gregory_%281440%29_by_Robert_Campin.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1812" data-file-height="2200" /></a><figcaption>The <i><a href="/wiki/Mass_of_St._Gregory" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass of St. Gregory">Mass of St. Gregory</a></i>, by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Campin" title="Robert Campin">Robert Campin</a>, 15th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>The late medieval subject of the <a href="/wiki/Mass_of_St._Gregory" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass of St. Gregory">Mass of St. Gregory</a> shows a version of a 7th-century story that was elaborated in later <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a>. Gregory is shown saying Mass when Christ as the <a href="/wiki/Man_of_Sorrows" title="Man of Sorrows">Man of Sorrows</a> appears on the altar. The subject was most common in the 15th and 16th centuries, and reflected growing emphasis on the <a href="/wiki/Real_Presence" class="mw-redirect" title="Real Presence">Real Presence</a>, and after the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a> was an assertion of the doctrine against Protestant theology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERubin1991308_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERubin1991308-80">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Famous_quotes_and_anecdotes">Famous quotes and anecdotes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Gregory_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Famous quotes and anecdotes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg/170px-Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="197" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg/255px-Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg/340px-Westminster_Cathedral_Non_Angli_sed_Angeli_si_Christiani.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2196" data-file-height="2539" /></a><figcaption>19th-century <a href="/wiki/Mosaic" title="Mosaic">mosaic</a> in <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Cathedral" title="Westminster Cathedral">Westminster Cathedral</a>, <i>Non Angli sed Angeli</i></figcaption></figure>
<ul><li><i>Non <strong>Angli</strong>, sed <strong>angeli</strong>, si forent Christiani.</i>– "They are not <a href="/wiki/Angles_(tribe)" title="Angles (tribe)">Angles</a>, but <a href="/wiki/Angel" title="Angel">angels</a>, if they were Christian".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZuckermann2003117_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuckermann2003117-81">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Aphorism" title="Aphorism">Aphorism</a>, summarizing words reported to have been spoken by Gregory when he first encountered pale-skinned English boys at a <a href="/wiki/Slave_market" title="Slave market">slave market</a>, sparking his dispatch of <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Canterbury" title="Augustine of Canterbury">St. Augustine of Canterbury</a> to England to convert the English, according to <a href="/wiki/Bede" title="Bede">Bede</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBede1999Book_II_Ch._I_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBede1999Book_II_Ch._I-82">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> He said: "Well named, for they have angelic faces and ought to be co-heirs with the angels in heaven."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHuntPoole1905115_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuntPoole1905115-83">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Discovering that their province was <a href="/wiki/Deira" title="Deira">Deira</a>, he went on to add that they would be rescued <i>de ira</i>, "from the wrath", and that their king was named <a href="/wiki/%C3%86lla_of_Deira" title="Ælla of Deira">Aella</a>, <i>Alleluia</i>, he said.<sup id="cite_ref-Earliest_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Earliest-84">&#91;k&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Locusta</i>, literally, "<a href="/wiki/Locust" title="Locust">locust</a>". However, the word sounds very much like "<i>loco sta</i>", meaning, "Stay in place!" Gregory himself wanted to go to England as a missionary and started out for there. On the fourth day of the journey, as they stopped for lunch, a locust landed on the edge of the Bible which Gregory was reading. He exclaimed, <i>locusta!</i> (locust). Reflecting on it, he understood it as a sign from Heaven whereby God wanted him to <i>loco sta</i>, that is, remain in his own place. Within the hour an emissary of the pope<sup id="cite_ref-BenPel_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BenPel-85">&#91;l&#93;</a></sup> arrived to recall him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHuntPoole1905115_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuntPoole1905115-83">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>"I beg that you will not take the present amiss. For anything, however trifling, which is offered from the prosperity of St. Peter should be regarded as a great blessing, seeing that he will have power both to bestow on you greater things, and to hold out to you eternal benefits with Almighty God."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li>
<li><i>Pro cuius amore in eius eloquio nec mihi parco</i> – "For the love of whom (God) I do not spare myself from His Word."<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregoryn.d.Cod._Sang._211_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregoryn.d.Cod._Sang._211-87">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The sense is that since the creator of the human race and redeemer of him unworthy gave him the power of the tongue so that he could witness, what kind of a witness would he be if he did not use it but preferred to speak infirmly?</li>
<li>"For the place of heretics is very pride itself...for the place of the wicked is pride just as conversely humility is the place of the good."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards1980228_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards1980228-48">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>"Whoever calls himself universal bishop, or desires this title, is, by his pride, the precursor to the Antichrist."<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Non enim pro locis res, sed pro bonis rebus loca amanda sunt</i> – "Things are not to be loved for the sake of a place, but places are to be loved for the sake of their good things." When Augustine asked whether to use <a href="/wiki/Roman_Rite" title="Roman Rite">Roman</a> or <a href="/wiki/Gallican_Rite" title="Gallican Rite">Gallican</a> customs in the Mass in England, Gregory said, in paraphrase, that it was not the place that imparted goodness but good things that graced the place, and it was more important to be pleasing to the Almighty. They should pick out what was "pia", "religiosa" and "recta" from any church whatever and set that down before the English minds as practice.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBede1999Book_I_section_27_part_II_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBede1999Book_I_section_27_part_II-89">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>"For the rule of justice and reason suggests that one who desires his own orders to be observed by his successors should undoubtedly keep the will and ordinances of his predecessor."<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> In his letters, Gregory often emphasized the importance of giving proper deference to last wills and testaments, and of respecting property rights.</li>
<li>"Compassion should be shown first to the faithful and afterwards to the enemies of the church."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards1980232_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards1980232-91">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>"At length being anxious to avoid all these inconveniences, I sought the haven of the monastery...
